## Assistant (Plan · DeepSeek V4 Flash Free · 19.5s)

_Thinking:_

I have enough from the key code. The core update in `mo_solve_nonhydro.f90` around line 1367:

```
p_nh%prog(nnew)%vn(je,jk,jb) = p_nh%prog(nnow)%vn(je,jk,jb) + dtime * z_ddt_vn_dyn
```

where
```
z_ddt_vn_dyn = z_ddt_vn_apc (advection + Coriolis) + z_ddt_vn_pgr (pressure gradient) + p_nh%diag%ddt_vn_phy (physics applied in dynamics)
```

So the characterization `u^{n+1} = u^n + Δt[(du/dt)_dynamics + (du/dt)_GWD + (du/dt)_turbulence + ...]` is roughly accurate in that physics tendencies enter as additive tendencies in the same time step. But there are important caveats:

1. The time stepping is not a simple forward Euler — it's a predictor-corrector (two-stage) scheme with semi-implicit treatment of fast waves. Line 421: "istep == 1 predictor step".

2. The GWD and turbulence tendencies are NOT directly evaluated for every u at the "dynamics" dt; they are computed in separate physics calls with their own time intervals (dt_gwd, dt_turb), and then combined. Actually, turbdiff both computes tendencies AND applies them directly (fast physics applied within the physics interface). Let me check.

Actually, from the turbdiff interface, turbulence tendencies are applied within the fast physics (p_diag%u += tcall_turb * ddt_u_turb). This happens in the fast physics rather than being passed to dynamics as ddt.

In the NWP model, there's a split: "fast physics" (turbulence, SSO/GWD often done in a way where they're directly applied/relaxation) vs "slow physics" (radiation, convection) which are added as tendencies to the dynamics driver.

Let me check what ddt_vn_phy contains and how it's assembled in mo_nh_interface_nwp.f90.

Let me look at that. Also, there's the point that the dynamics time step involves substepping (r_nsubsteps), time weights (wgt_nnow_vel, wgt_nnew_vel), so ddt_vn_phy is evaluated at the appropriate time level.

The key caveats for the characterization:

1. ICON dynamical core uses a **two-stage predictor-corrector with semi-implicit time integration** (not simple explicit Euler). Physics tendencies enter in both predictor and corrector steps (with time weighting).

2. **Not all physics is added as "tendencies in the momentum equation"**. In the ICON NWP setup, turbulence (turbdiff/turbtrans) and microphysics are "fast physics" applied directly within the physics interface (they update state directly: p_diag%u += dt*ddt_u_turb). Slow physics (radiation, convection, GWD?) produce tendencies that are passed to dynamics as ddt_vn_phy, added to the dynamics driver `solve_nh`.

This is very informative. So in the NWP physics, the wind tendencies that go into `ddt_vn_phy` (slow physics) are: GWD + Rayleigh friction + SSO + convection (pconv). Turbulence (ddt_u_turb) is applied directly to vn state in the interface (`pt_prog%vn = pt_prog%vn + dt_loc * turb tendency`) — it's a "fast physics" treatment.

I already have strong evidence:
- `nwp_nh_interface` line 2454: z_ddt_u_tot = ddt_u_gwd + raylfric + ddt_u_sso + ddt_u_pconv
- Turbulence (`ddt_u_*_turb`) is applied directly (`pt_prog%vn += dt_loc * ...` lines 2855-2894) and also contributes to ddt_vn_phy via z_ddt_u_tot if l_any_slowphys - actually wait, in the first branch (line 2844) ddt_vn_phy is computed with z_ddt_u_tot (which contains gwd+sso+pconv+raylfric), and then ALSO the turb tendency is added separately to pt_prog%vn. So turbulence is applied directly (fast physics) whereas slow physics (rad, conv, gwd, sso) enters as ddt_vn_phy within the dynamics time step.

So the characterization u^{n+1} = u^n + Δt[dynamics + GWD + turbulence + ...] is roughly correct conceptually: physics tendencies are added to the dynamics tendencies and the whole thing scaled by Δt. But there are important caveats:

1. The time stepping in ICON dynamics is a two-step predictor-corrector (istep=1,2), semi-implicit treatment of fast waves (horizontal pressure gradient & divergence are treated semi-implicitly). So it's NOT a single explicit forward Euler step; the dynamics themselves are a predictor-corrector with time-averaged tendencies (wgt_nnow_vel/wgt_nnew_vel).

2. The velocity update equation shown (vn at edges) is exactly: vn(nnew) = vn(nnow) + dtime * (advection+coriolis + pressure gradient + physics(ddt_vn_phy)). So yes for vn.

3. Turbulence is a "fast physics" applied directly to the state (vn += dt_loc * ddt_vn_turb) rather than through ddt_vn_phy in the same way. Actually it's still an additive. But the velocities: turbulence tendency added directly in nwp_nh_interface. GWD/SSO/conv/rad are slow physics entering via ddt_vn_phy inside solve_nh.

So the answer: roughly yes, it's an additive tendency split. Caveats:
- It's not plain forward Euler; the dynamics solve is a two-stage predictor-corrector (semi-implicit) with time weights.
- Physics: some "fast" physics (turbulence, and partially microphysics) are applied directly to the prognostic state (additive), while "slow" physics (radiation, convection, GWD/SSO, Rayleigh friction) are accumulated as ddt_vn_phy and folded into the dynamics tendency, then scaled by the full dtime. All are additive tendencies applied with Δt.
- Also there are substeps (r_nsubsteps), divergence damping, Rayleigh, IAU, etc. added on top.
- For variables other than wind (theta_v, rho, tracers), similar.

So the characterization is essentially right at the conceptual level — it's an additive operator split where physics tendencies are added to the dynamics tendencies each time step. Worth noting the details. Let me be concise but informative.

This is plan mode, but this is really a question, not a task to modify files. I should just answer. Keep it reasonably concise. The user asked "is this roughly accurate?" — the answer is yes, roughly, with caveats. Let me give a focused answer referencing code lines.

I should not create a plan since there's nothing to implement. This is a Q&A. In plan mode I answer the question.

Yes, roughly accurate for the horizontal wind equation — with a few important caveats.

The additive-tendency structure you wrote is exactly what ICON does. In `mo_solve_nonhydro.f90:1363-1367` the velocity update is:

```
vn(nnew) = vn(nnow) + dtime * (advection+Coriolis + pressure-gradient + ddt_vn_phy)
```

where `ddt_vn_phy` is the summed physics tendency passed from `nwp_nh_interface` (mo_nh_interface_nwp.f90:2454-2457):

```
ddt_u_tot = ddt_u_gwd + Rayleigh-friction + ddt_u_sso + ddt_u_pconv
```

Caveats:

1. **It's not forward Euler.** The dynamics is a two-stage predictor–corrector (`DO istep = 1,2` in mo_solve_nonhydro.f90:419), with time-averaged advection terms (`wgt_nnow_vel`/`wgt_nnew_vel`, line 409-410) and semi-implicit treatment of the fast pressure-gradient/divergence modes. The physics tendency `ddt_vn_phy` is folded into both the predictor (line 1407-1410) and corrector (line 1367) steps.

2. **Turbulence enters differently.** `ddt_u_turb` is applied directly to `vn` in the physics interface (`pt_prog%vn += dt_loc * ...`, mo_nh_interface_nwp.f90:2855-2865), not through `ddt_vn_phy`. So your box is still an additive update, but turbulence is "fast physics," applied straight to the state, whereas GWD/SSO/Rayleigh/conv/radiation are "slow physics" accumulated into `ddt_vn_phy`.

3. **Extra terms** on top of the pure RHS: divergence damping, Rayleigh fricition, IAU (initialization) tendencies, and multiple substeps (`r_nsubsteps`) — all just additional additive tendencies scaled by Δt.

So: conceptually correct (physics = additive tendencies added to dynamics tendencies, all scaled by Δt), but the dynamics side is a predictor–corrector semi-implicit scheme, not a single explicit Euler step, and turbulence is split out as fast physics rather than passing through the same `ddt_vn_phy` channel.

`vn` in ICON is the horizontal velocity component normal to edges. It's the prognostic horizontal wind component. The "vn_dyn" is the tendency `ddt_vn_dyn` which aggregates the total dynamics tendency.

## Assistant (Plan · DeepSeek V4 Flash Free · 50.2s)

_Thinking:_

Now I have clear answer. Let me confirm what `vn` is - the normal velocity component at edges (the prognostic horizontal wind in ICON's C-grid / unpreconditioned scheme). And `ddt_vn_dyn` is the total dynamics tendency of that, being the sum of component tendencies.

Let me answer concisely.

No — `vn` is a wind *component*, not a tendency; `ddt_vn_dyn` is the tendency of it.

**`vn`** is the prognostic horizontal wind in ICON: the velocity component **normal (perpendicular) to each triangular-mesh edge** (a C-grid / triangulated "unpreconditioned" discretization), with `vt` being the tangential component at the same edge (mo_nonhydro_types.f90:155,161). Units: m/s. It's the state variable that gets updated in the momentum equation.

**`ddt_vn_dyn`** is the **total dynamics tendency** of `vn`, i.e. the full RHS of the horizontal-momentum equation, and per the declaration it is "the sum of the following contributions" (mo_nonhydro_types.f90:185,188-214):

```
ddt_vn_dyn = ddt_vn_adv (advection)
           + ddt_vn_cor (Coriolis)
           + ddt_vn_pgr (pressure gradient)
           + ddt_vn_phd (physics applied in dynamics)
           + ddt_vn_dmp (divergence damping)
           + ddt_vn_hdf (horizontal diffusion)
           + ddt_vn_ray (Rayleigh damping)
           + ddt_vn_iau (incremental analysis update)
           + ddt_vn_grf (grid-refinement tendency)
```

These pieces are accumulated in `mo_solve_nonhydro.f90` (e.g. line 1393 adds `z_ddt_vn_dyn`, then damping at 1468/1498/1521, IAU at 1549, Rayleigh at 1580, grid-refinement at 1620). The update itself is `vn(nnew) = vn(nnow) + dtime * ddt_vn_dyn` (line 1367).

Note: `ddt_vn_dyn` is an **optional diagnostic** — it's only computed/allocated if you request `ddt_vn_dyn` in the output (`ddt_vn_dyn_is_associated`, set in mo_io_config.f90:200/419). It is a diagnosed total, not something the integrator needs.

## Assistant (Plan · DeepSeek V4 Flash Free · 57.4s)

_Thinking:_

The user asks about the difference between z_ddt_u_tot and z_ddt_v_tot. They are the zonal and meridional horizontal wind tendencies (u and v components). Let me verify from the code.

From the earlier grep:
- `z_ddt_u_tot(jc,jk,jb) = prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_gwd(jc,jk,jb) + zddt_u_raylfric(jc,jk) + prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_sso(jc,jk,jb) + prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_pconv(jc,jk,jb)`
- `z_ddt_v_tot(jc,jk,jb) = prm_nwp_tend%ddt_v_gwd(jc,jk,jb) + zddt_v_raylfric(jc,jk) + prm_nwp_tend%ddt_v_sso(jc,jk,jb) + prm_nwp_tend%ddt_v_pconv(jc,jk,jb)`

So they are the total slow-physics zonal (u, eastward, x) and meridional (v, northward, y) wind tendencies. u and v are the Cartesian projections of horizontal velocity at cell centers. Both are "horizontal tendencies" but for the two horizontal directions/east-north components of the wind vector.

Let me check where u/v are defined — they're the zonal/meridional components. Let me confirm.

## Assistant (Plan · DeepSeek V4 Flash Free · 41.9s)

_Thinking:_

Let me confirm the u/v definition. The tendencies are zonal and meridional wind components. Let me check where u and v are defined in diagnostics.

They're the **zonal** and **meridional** wind tendency components, i.e. the two horizontal directions of the wind vector — not two different schemes.

- `z_ddt_u_tot = total slow-physics tendency of u`, the **zonal** wind (east–west) component, at cell centers.
- `z_ddt_v_tot = total slow-physics tendency of v`, the **meridional** (north–south) component.

The comment at mo_nh_interface_nwp.f90:279 calls them "hor. wind tendencies" collectively, but each contains the same four schemes summed for its own component (lines 2454-2457):

```
z_ddt_u_tot = ddt_u_gwd + u_rayleigh + ddt_u_sso + ddt_u_pconv   (zonal)
z_ddt_v_tot = ddt_v_gwd + v_rayleigh + ddt_v_sso + ddt_v_pconv   (meridional)
```

This mirrors the underlying physics fields, which are declared as "ZonalW-tendency"/"MeridW-tendency" (mo_nwp_phy_types.f90:607-626). Both together make up the full 2D horizontal wind tendency vector `(∂u/∂t, ∂v/∂t)`; they're later interpolated onto edge-normal components as `ddt_vn_phy` (mo_nh_interface_nwp.f90:2844-2853).

- `z` = local (temporary/automatic) real variables or arrays (work fields)

In COSMO/ICON coding standards:
- Variables are named by type and scope. The prefixes commonly used:
  - `n` = integer (namelist-like, e.g. numbers)
  - `i` = integer index/loop variable
  - `l` = logical
  - `r` = real
  - `z` = real 3D/2D/1D automatic arrays or local scalars — the "z" stands for... In COSMO convention, `z` was used for real local variables/arrays (from German "Zwischen" meaning intermediate/temporary, or just an arbitrary type letter).
  - `p` = pointer
  - `t` = type/derived type
  - `pt_` = pointer to type

The `z` in DWD codes: In the ICON codebase, variables starting with `z` are local automatic arrays and scalars used as working storage. Actually in ICON specifically, I remember `z_` prefix is used for local variables while `i_`, `l_`, `r_` etc. Let me check the actual modern ICON code.

Looking at code: `z_ddt_u_tot`, `z_exner_sv`, `z_tempv`, `sqrt_ri`, `n2`, `dvdz2`, `wfac`, `zcosmu0`, `z_qsum`, `z_ddt_temp_drag` — all local variables declared within the subroutine.

Actually the DWD ICON coding standard: naming convention documented in the ICON/coding standards: 

From the ICON coding standards (DWD): variable naming follows:
- The prefix letter indicates the data type:
  - `i` / `j` / `k` : INTEGER indices
  - `l` : LOGICAL
  - `r` : REAL scalar
  - `z` : REAL array (local variables and arrays)
  - `n` : INTEGER (non-index)
  - `p` : POINTER
  - `t` : TYPE
  - `pt_` : POINTER to TYPE
  - `c` : CHARACTER
  - `s` : CHARACTER (string)
  - `c_` ... 

Yes! This is the standard DWD COSMO/ICON convention: The first letter encodes the type:
- `i`, `j`, `k` for INTEGER loop counters
- `n` for INTEGER counts/indices
- `l` for LOGICAL
- `r` for REAL scalars
- `z` for REAL arrays and local workspace (both scalar and array in ICON actual practice — but historically in COSMO `z` was specifically real work arrays)
- `c` for CHARACTER
- `p` for POINTER
- `t` for TYPE

Actually in the ICON code I see `z_` used for real local arrays AND scalars (e.g., `z_tempv` real scalar, `zcosmu0` array, `zsct` scalar). So `z` = local REAL variables (work), while `r` sometimes used for real constants/scalars passed... hmm.

So the answer: `z` is a type-prefix in the DWD Fortran naming convention — it denotes a local REAL (typically a temporary/work) variable or array, as opposed to e.g. `i` (integer), `l` (logical), `p` (pointer), `t` (type). It helps distinguish local scratch memory from the actual physical tendency fields `ddt_u_*`, and from pointer/argument variables.

Actually in DWD's coding standard (from COSMO heritage), the convention is:
- Local variables/arrays are prefixed with `z` (from German "zwischen" = temporary, or just random letter). 
- Variables of the patch/state types use `p_`
- Physics tendency fields use `prm_nwp_tend%`
- Loop indices use `j`, `k`
- Integer counts use `i_` or `n_`
- Logical flags use `l_`

Actually, in the COSMO model coding convention (which ICON inherits), the letter codes are:

From the DWD "COSMO Programmierung" docs: prefix `z` denotes "temporäre (automatische) Felder und Variablen" - temporary (automatic) fields and variables, i.e., local workspace variables. This is why `z_ddt_u_tot`, `z_ddt_temp`, `z_exner_sv`, `z_tempv` are all local to `nwp_nh_interface`.

Yes, `z` = "Zwischengrößen" (intermediate quantities) essentially - local working variables. This is the standard DWD/COSMO/ICON convention distinguishing locals from:

I'm in plan mode - but this is really just a question, not a task requiring edits. I should answer the question. Plan mode forbids edits; answering a question is fine.

The `z` prefix is a **DWD/COSMO/ICON Fortran coding-standard convention for local (subroutine-scoped) working variables**. It doesn't denote a physical meaning like "zonal" — it flags that the variable is scratch workspace, valid only inside the current subroutine.

Evidence from the declaration block in `nwp_nh_interface` (mo_nh_interface_nwp.f90:277-280):

```
REAL(wp), TARGET :: &                   !> temporal arrays for
  & z_ddt_u_tot (nproma,...),&
  & z_ddt_v_tot (nproma,...),&          !< hor. wind tendencies
  & z_ddt_temp  (nproma,pt_patch%nlev)  !< Temperature tendency
```

So `z_ddt_u_tot`/`z_ddt_v_tot` are **local scratch arrays** that temporarily accumulate the slow-physics wind tendencies before interpolation onto edge normals. They are not persisted diagnostics.

The point of the convention: it visually separates three classes of variables in one routine:
- `z_*` — local temporaries (e.g. `z_ddt_u_tot`, `z_exner_sv`, `z_tempv`, `z_qsum`)
- `pt_*` / `p_*` — subroutine arguments/pointers
- module-state fields — accessed via `prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_gwd`, etc.

That's why the "real" tendency fields you asked about earlier (`ddt_u_gwd`, `ddt_u_sso`, `ddt_u_pconv`) live in the `prm_nwp_tend` container with no `z` prefix — they're the persistent physics-state tendencies — while `z_ddt_u_tot` is just the ephemeral local sum of them.

More detailed scientific issues can be found in the mentioned literature references.\n\n**3\\.2 SET-UP**\n\nThe full linearized physics package currently in operational use is activated by setting the following switches in namelist NAMTRAJP:\n• LEVDIF2 (vertical diffusion),\n\n• LEGWDG2 (subgrid-scale orographic gravity wave drag),\n\n• LEGWWMS2 (non-orographic gravity wave drag),\n\n• LERADI2, LERADS, LERADN2 and LERADSW2 (radiation),\n\n• LENCLD2 (large-scale condensation/precipitation),\n\n• LECUMF2 and LECUMFS2 (in namelist NAMCUMFS) (moist convection),\n\nIn addition, the following switches must be set to .TRUE.: LEPHYS and LAGPHY (also necessary to activate the ECMWF non-linear physics) in namelist NAEPHY and LETRAJP (to activate storage of the trajectory at t − ∆t) in namelist NAMTRAJP.\n\nTunable parameters of the improved physics (which should not in principle be modified) are defined in SUPHLI. The logical LPHYLIN (namelist NAMTLEVOL)is used to activate the simplifications and/or modifications associated with the linear package in the non-linear physics. This variable is set to .FALSE. by default, but is forced to .TRUE. before starting tangent-linear (TL) and adjoint (AD) computations in CNT3TL and CNT3AD.\n\n...\n\nAs mentioned above, the representation of wave breaking is currently not activated in the linearized code by setting the constant RLPDRAG to zero in SUPHLI. Note that RLPDRAG is only used when logical LPHYLIN is .TRUE., otherwise GKDRAG is used (set to 0.3 in SUGWD).\n\n**3\\.6 NON-OROGRAPHIC GRAVITY WAVE DRAG**\n\nThe non-linear scheme for non-orographic gravity wave (nonorog-gw) (Orr et al. (2010)) has been used operationally in the forecast model since September 2009 (cycle 35R3). Tangent-linear and adjoint versions were developed in order to reduce discrepancies between the full non-linear and linearized versions of the model. This parametrization scheme is based on Scinocca (2003). The latter is a spectral scheme derived from the Warner and McIntyre (1996) scheme which represents the three basic mechanisms of conservative propagation, critical level filtering, and non-linear dissipation.\nSince the full non-hydrostatic and rotational wave dynamics considered by Warner and McIntyre (1996) is too costly for operational models, only hydrostatic and non-rotational wave dynamics are employed.\n\nThe dispersion relation for a hydrostatic gravity wave in the absence of rotation is\n\nm ² = k ² N ²\n\n˜ _ω_ ² = N ²\n\n˜c ² (3.14)\n\nwhere k, m are horizontal and vertical wavenumbers, while ˜ _ω_ = _ω_ − kU and ˜c = c − U are the intrinsic frequency and phase speed (with c being the ground based phase speed and U the background wind speed in the direction of propagation), respectively.\n\nIFS Documentation – Cy49r1 19\n\nChapter 3: Tangent-linear physics\n\nThe launch spectrum, which is globally uniform and constant, is given by the total wave energy per unit mass in each azimuth angle _φ_ following Fritts and Nastrom (1993) as\n\n˜E(m, ˜ _ω_ , _φ_ ) = B  m\n\nm∗\n\ns N ² ˜ _ω_ − d\n\n1 −  m\n\nm∗ s+3 (3.15)\nwhere B, s and d are constants, and m∗ = 2 _π_ L is a transitional wavelength. Instead of the total wave energy ˜ E(m, ˜ _ω_ , _φ_ ), the momentum flux spectral density _ρ_ ˜ F(m, ˜ _ω_ , _φ_ ) is required. This is obtained through the group velocity rule. In order to have the momentum flux conserved in the absence of dissipative processes as the spectrum propagates vertically through height-varying background wind and buoyancy frequency, the coordinate framework (k, _ω_ ) is used instead of (m, ˜ _ω_ ) as shown in Scinocca (2003).\n\nThe dissipative mechanisms applied to the wave field in each azimuthal direction and on each model level are critical level filtering and non-linear dissipation. After application of them, the resulting momentum flux profiles are used to derive the net eastward _ρ_ ¯ FE and northward _ρ_ ¯ FN fluxes. The tendencies for the (u, v) wind components are then given by the divergence of those fluxes obtained through summation of the total momentum flux (i.e.\nintegrated over all phase speed bins) in each azimuth _φ_ i projected onto the east and north directions, respectively:\n\n_∂_ u _∂_ t = g _∂_ ( _ρ_ ¯ FE )\n\n_∂_ p (3.16)\n\n_∂_ v _∂_ t = g _∂_ ( _ρ_ ¯ FN )\n\n_∂_ p (3.17)\n\nwhere g is the gravitational acceleration and p is the pressure.\n\nIn order to eliminate the artificial noise in TL computations caused by the introduction of this scheme, it was necessary to implement some regularizations. Generally,\n\nmore complex networks produce more accurate emulators. By training on an increased\n\ncomplexity version of the existing parameterisation scheme we build emulators that\n\nproduce more accurate forecasts. For medium range forecasting we find evidence our\n\nemulators are more accurate than the version of the parametrisation scheme that is\n\nused for operational predictions. Using the current operational CPU hardware our\n\nemulators have a similar computational cost to the existing scheme, but are heavily\n\nlimited by data movement. On GPU hardware our emulators perform ten times faster\n\nthan the existing scheme on a CPU.\n\n**Plain Language Summary**\n\n...\n\nmanuscript submitted to _Journal of Advances in Modeling Earth Systems_\n\nor vertical grids and aqua-planet configurations. Here we will assess our emulators\n\nwhen coupled to models running on the 25km grid used for long-range forecasting at\n\nECMWF, a significant step towards operational forecasting.\n\nGravity wave drag is a parameterised physical process that has not yet been con-\n\nsidered for emulation. Orographic gravity wave drag is discussed\n\nin section 5. Non-orographic gravity waves can be generated by dynamics such as\n\nfronts and convection and occur on a vast range of scales (Gardner et al., 1989; Ern\n\net al., 2004) meaning that while some gravity waves are resolved by current forecast\n\nresolutions, others need to be parameterised. In the IFS model this is achieved using\nthe scheme from Warner and McIntyre (2001) and in particular the variant described\n\nin Orr et al. (2010) (henceforth referred to as NOGWD). In this scheme a fixed amount of\n\nmomentum is launched upwards from a given height at a range of launch angles in\n\nthe horizontal direction and over a range of phase speeds. At each model level the\n\nstability of these waves within the atmospheric profile is calculated, and depending\n\non this stability momentum is deposited at these layers. Accurate parameterisation\n\nof non-orographic gravity wave drag is important for maintaining an accurate zonal-\n\nmean wind and temperature distribution (Garcia & Boville, 1994) and for capturing\n\nthe phase and amplitude of the Quasi-Biennial Oscillation (QBO) (Dunkerton, 1997).\n\n...\n\nour machine learning methodology (section 3). We\n\n–22–\n\nmanuscript submitted to _Journal of Advances in Modeling Earth Systems_\n\nused the same methodology for data generation, same normalisation methods and same\n\nnetwork architecture choices. Orographic gravity wave drag shares the same inputs\n\nas its non-orographic counterpart, with the addition of four parameters describing the\n\nunresolved orography. The existing OGWD scheme is active only in locations with\n\nsignificant unresolved orography and when the atmospheric profile is susceptible to\n\nthe waves generated by that orography. The result of this is a scheme that is inactive\n\nfor most of the globe and is often inactive even in locations with significant unresolved\n\norography.\n\nUnfortunately, our attempts to learn this OGWD scheme with fully-connected\n\nneural networks were unsuccessful.\n\n...\n\ndata to exclude a proportion of our training data where the scheme was inactive, but\n\nthis often resulted in a dramatic increase in false positives (OGWD activity where the\n\ncurrent scheme is inactive).\n\nFrom this experience we summarise that training emulators of physical param-\n\neterisation schemes is a non-trivial task, not guaranteed to succeed even with large\n\namounts of available data. Our outlook is that most parameterisation schemes share\n\nmore in common with the NOGWD scheme and therefore will be possible to learn.\n\nHowever this remains to be tested.\n\n**6 Conclusions**\n\nIn this work we successfully emulate the non-orographic gravity wave drag scheme\n\nfrom the operational IFS forecasting model.         "In addition, in stably stratified flow, the orographic drag parametrization (Chapter 4) represents the effects of low-level blocking due to unresolved orography (blocked flow drag) and the absorption and/or reflection of vertically propagating gravity waves (gravity wave drag) on the momentum budget (Lott and Miller, 1997).\n\nThe non-orographic gravity wave drag parametrization (Chapter 5) accounts for the effects of unresolved non-orographic gravity waves. These waves are generated in nature by processes like deep convection, frontal disturbances, and shear zones. Propagating upward from the troposphere the waves break in the middle atmosphere, comprising the stratosphere and the mesosphere, where they exert a strong drag on the flow. The parametrization uses a globally uniform wave spectrum, and propagates it vertically through changing horizontal winds and air density, thereby representing the wave breaking effects due to critical level filtering and non-linear dissipation.\n\n...\n\n16) is evaluated at the updated time _t_ \\+ d _t_ , while the wind amplitude, _|_ _U_ _|_ , is evaluated at the previous time step.\n\n**4\\.2.2 Gravity-wave drag**\n\nThis gravity-wave part of the scheme is based on the work of Miller _et al._ (1989) and Baines and Palmer (1990), and takes into account some three-dimensional effects in the wave stress amplitude and orientation. For clarity and convenience, a brief description is given here. ! (C) Copyright 1989- ECMWF.
```
! This software is licensed under the terms of the Apache Licence Version 2.0
! which can be obtained at http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0.

SUBROUTINE GWDRAG_WMS(YDSTA,YDEGWD,YDEGWWMS,KIDIA,  KFDIA,   KLON,  KLEV,   KLAUNCH, PTSTEP,&
                     &PTM1 ,  PUM1,    PVM1,  PAPM1,  PAPHM1, PGEO1 ,&
                     &PGELAT, PGAW,    PPRECIP,&
                     &PTENU,  PTENV,   PFLUXU, PFLUXV)

!**** *GWDRAG_WMS*  MASTER ROUTINE FOR CUMULUS MASSFLUX-SCHEME

!     Original Fortran Code by   J. SCINOCCIA
!     Rewritten in IFS format by A. ORR          E.C.M.W.F.     August 2008

!     PURPOSE
!     -------

!          THIS ROUTINE COMPUTES NON-OROGRAPHIC GRAVITY WAVE DRAG
!     AFTER SCINOCCA (2003) AND Mc LANDRESS AND SCINOCCIA (JAS 2005)
!     HYDROSTATIC NON-ROTATIONAL SIMPLIFIED VERSION OF THE 
!     WARNER AND MCINTYRE (1996) NON-OROGRAPHIC GRAVITY WAVE PARAMETERIZATION 
!     CONSTANTS HAVE BEEN OPTIMIZED FOLLOWING M. ERN ET AL. (ATMOS. CHEM. PHYS. 2006)

!     REFERENCE: Orr, A., P. Bechtold, J. Scinoccia, M. Ern, M. Janiskova, 2010: 
!                Improved middle atmosphere climate and analysis in the ECMWF forecasting system 
!                through a non-orographic gravity wave parametrization. J.  Climate., 23, 5905-5926.

!     LAUNCH SPECTRUM - GENERALIZED DESAUBIES 
!     INCLUDES A CRITICAL-LEVEL CORRECTION THAT PREVENTS THE 
!     MOMEMTUM DEPOSITION IN EACH AZIMUTH FROM DRIVING THE FLOW TO SPEEDS FASTER 
!     THAN THE PHASE SPEED OF THE WAVES, I.E. WHEN WAVES BREAK THEY DRAG THE MEAN 
!     FLOW TOWARDS THEIR PHASE SPEED - NOT PAST IT.  

!**   INTERFACE.
!     ----------
!          *GWDRAG_WMS* IS CALLED FROM *CALLPAR*

!     MODIFICATIONS
!     -------------
!           October 2008 : Cleaning and full adaptation   P. Bechtold/JJMorcrette
!                          Optimisation+bug corrections   P. Bechtold
!           November 2012: possibility of different ZGAM transform T. Stockdale
!           November 2015: resolution scaling for octahedral grid  P. Bechtold
! ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------


USE YOMSTA     , ONLY : TSTA
USE PARKIND1,    ONLY : JPIM, JPRB
USE YOEGWWMS,    ONLY : TEGWWMS
USE YOMCST,      ONLY : RG, RD, RCPD, RPI, RA
USE YOMHOOK,     ONLY : LHOOK,   DR_HOOK, JPHOOK
USE YOEGWD,      ONLY : TEGWD

IMPLICIT NONE

!in
TYPE(TSTA)        ,INTENT(IN) :: YDSTA
TYPE(TEGWD)       ,INTENT(INOUT):: YDEGWD
TYPE(TEGWWMS)     ,INTENT(INOUT):: YDEGWWMS
INTEGER(KIND=JPIM),INTENT(IN) :: KIDIA, KFDIA
INTEGER(KIND=JPIM),INTENT(IN) :: KLON                 ! horizontal dimension
INTEGER(KIND=JPIM),INTENT(IN) :: KLEV                 ! vertical levels
INTEGER(KIND=JPIM),INTENT(IN) :: KLAUNCH              ! index for launch level 
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(IN) :: PTSTEP               ! model time step
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(IN) :: PUM1(KLON,KLEV)      ! full model level zonal velocity (t-dt)
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(IN) :: PVM1(KLON,KLEV)      ! full model level meridional velocity (t-dt)
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(IN) :: PTM1(KLON,KLEV)      ! full model level temperature (t-dt)
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(IN) :: PAPM1(KLON,KLEV)     ! full model level pressure (t-dt)
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(IN) :: PAPHM1(KLON,KLEV+1)  ! half-model level pressure (t-dt)
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(IN) :: PGEO1(KLON,KLEV)     ! full model level geopotential
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(IN) :: PGELAT(KLON)         ! latitude
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(IN) :: PGAW(KLON)           ! normalised gaussian quadrature weight/number of longitude points
                                                      ! local sub-area == 4*RPI*RA**2 * PGAW
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(IN) :: PPRECIP(KLON)        ! total surface precipitation

!out
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(OUT):: PTENU(KLON,KLEV)     ! full-model level zonal momentum tendency
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(OUT):: PTENV(KLON,KLEV)     ! full-model level meridional momentum tendency
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(OUT):: PFLUXU(KLON,KLEV+1)  ! = zonal component of vertical momentum flux (Pa)  
REAL(KIND=JPRB)   ,INTENT(OUT):: PFLUXV(KLON,KLEV+1)  ! = meridional component of vertical momentum flux (Pa)
!work
INTEGER(KIND=JPIM), PARAMETER  :: IAZIDIM=4     !number of azimuths
INTEGER(KIND=JPIM), PARAMETER  :: INCDIM=20     !number of discretized c spectral elements in launch spectrum  

REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZUHM1(KLON,KLEV)             !half-model level zonal velocity
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZVHM1(KLON,KLEV)             !half-model level meridional velocity
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZBVFHM1(KLON,KLEV)           !half-model level Brunt-Vaisalla frequency
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZRHOHM1(KLON,KLEV)           !half-model level density
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZX(INCDIM)                   !coordinate transformation
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZCI(INCDIM)                  !phase speed element
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZDCI(INCDIM)
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZUI(KLON,KLEV,IAZIDIM)       !intrinsic velocity
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZUL(KLON,IAZIDIM)            !velocity in azimuthal direction at launch level
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZBVFL(KLON)                  !buoyancy at launch level
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZCOSANG(IAZIDIM)             !cos of azimuth angle
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZSINANG(IAZIDIM)             !sin of azimuth angle
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZFCT(KLON,KLEV)
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZFNORM(KLON)                 !normalisation factor (A)
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZCI_MIN(KLON,IAZIDIM)
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZTHM1(KLON,KLEV)             !temperature on half-model levels
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZFLUX(KLON,INCDIM,IAZIDIM)   !momentum flux at each vertical level and azimuth 
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZPU(KLON,KLEV,IAZIDIM)       !momentum flux
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZDFL(KLON,KLEV,IAZIDIM)
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZACT(KLON,INCDIM,IAZIDIM)    !if =1 then critical level encountered  
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZACC(KLON,INCDIM,IAZIDIM)
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZCRT(KLON,KLEV,IAZIDIM)

INTEGER(KIND=JPIM) :: ILAUNCH                   !model level from which GW spectrum is launched
INTEGER(KIND=JPIM) :: INC, JK, JL, IAZI
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZRADTODEG, ZGELATDEG
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZCIMIN, ZCIMAX
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZGAM, ZPEXP, ZXMAX, ZXMIN, ZXRAN, ZDX, ZX1, ZX2, ZDXA, ZDXB, ZDXS
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZANG, ZAZ_FCT, ZNORM, ZANG1, ZTX
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZU, ZCIN, ZCPEAK
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZCIN4, ZBVFL4, ZCIN2, ZBVFL2, ZCIN3, ZBVFL3, ZCINC
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZATMP, ZFLUXS, ZDEP, ZFLUXSQ, ZULM, ZDFT, ZE1, ZE2
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZMS_L,ZMS, Z0P5, Z0P0, Z50S
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZGAUSS(KLON), ZFLUXLAUN(KLON), ZCNGL(KLON)
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZCONS1,ZCONS2,ZDELP,ZRGPTS
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZTHSTD,ZRHOSTD,ZBVFSTD
REAL(KIND=JPRB) :: ZGAUSSB,ZFLUXGLOB

REAL(KIND=JPHOOK) :: ZHOOK_HANDLE

!--------------------------------------------------------------------------

IF (LHOOK) CALL DR_HOOK('GWDRAG_WMS',0,ZHOOK_HANDLE)
ASSOCIATE(GSSEC=>YDEGWD%GSSEC, &
 & GCOEFF=>YDEGWWMS%GCOEFF, GCSTAR=>YDEGWWMS%GCSTAR, &
 & GFLUXLAUNL=>YDEGWWMS%GFLUXLAUNL, GGAUSSA=>YDEGWWMS%GGAUSSA, &
 & GGAUSSB=>YDEGWWMS%GGAUSSB, GMSTAR_L=>YDEGWWMS%GMSTAR_L, &
 & GPTWO=>YDEGWWMS%GPTWO, LGACALC=>YDEGWWMS%LGACALC, LGINDL=>YDEGWWMS%LGINDL, &
 & LGSATL=>YDEGWWMS%LGSATL, LOZPR=>YDEGWWMS%LOZPR, NGAUSS=>YDEGWWMS%NGAUSS, &
 & NLAUNCHL=>YDEGWWMS%NLAUNCHL, NSLOPE=>YDEGWWMS%NSLOPE, &
 & STPHI=>YDSTA%STPHI, STPREH=>YDSTA%STPREH, STTEM=>YDSTA%STTEM)
!--------------------------------------------------------------------------

!*       INPUT PARAMETERS
!*       ---------------- 

ZRADTODEG=57.29577951_JPRB

! Set parameters which are a function of launch height
ILAUNCH=NLAUNCHL(KLAUNCH)
ZFLUXGLOB=GFLUXLAUNL(KLAUNCH)
ZGAUSSB=GGAUSSB(KLAUNCH)

ZMS_L=GMSTAR_L(KLAUNCH)
ZMS=2*RPI/ZMS_L    

!*       INITIALIZE FIELDS TO ZERO
!*       ------------------------- 

PTENU(:,:)=0.0_JPRB
PTENV(:,:)=0.0_JPRB

DO IAZI=1,IAZIDIM
   DO JK=1,KLEV
      DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
         ZPU(JL,JK,IAZI)=0.0_JPRB
         ZCRT(JL,JK,IAZI)=0.0_JPRB
         ZDFL(JL,JK,IAZI)=0.0_JPRB
      ENDDO
   ENDDO      
ENDDO         

DO JK=1,KLEV+1
   DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
     PFLUXU(JL,JK)=0.0_JPRB
     PFLUXV(JL,JK)=0.0_JPRB
   ENDDO
ENDDO


!*       INITIALIZE PARAMETERS FOR COORDINATE TRANSFORM
!*       ----------------------------------------------

! ZCIMIN,ZCIMAX - min,max intrinsic launch-level phase speed (c-U_o) (m/s)
! ZGAM - half=width of coordinate stretch

ZCIMIN=0.50_JPRB
ZCIMAX=100.0_JPRB
ZGAM=0.25_JPRB

ZPEXP=GPTWO/2.0_JPRB

! set initial min ci in each column and azimuth (used for critical levels)

DO IAZI=1,IAZIDIM
   DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
      ZCI_MIN(JL,IAZI)=ZCIMIN
   ENDDO
ENDDO

   
!*       DEFINE HALF MODEL LEVEL WINDS AND TEMPERATURE
!*       -----------------------------------

DO JK=2,KLEV
  DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA                                        
     ZTHM1(JL,JK) =0.5_JPRB*(PTM1(JL,JK-1)+PTM1(JL,JK)) 
     ZUHM1(JL,JK) =0.5_JPRB*(PUM1(JL,JK-1)+PUM1(JL,JK))
     ZVHM1(JL,JK) =0.5_JPRB*(PVM1(JL,JK-1)+PVM1(JL,JK))
  ENDDO
ENDDO
JK=1
DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA                                        
   ZTHM1(JL,JK)=PTM1(JL,JK) 
   ZUHM1(JL,JK)=PUM1(JL,JK)
   ZVHM1(JL,JK)=PVM1(JL,JK)
ENDDO


!*       DEFINE STATIC STABILITY AND AIR DENSITY ON HALF MODEL LEVELS
!*       ------------------------------------------------------------

ZCONS1=1.0_JPRB/RD
ZCONS2=RG**2/RCPD
DO JK=KLEV,2,-1
  DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
!    ZDELP=PAPM1(JL,JK)-PAPM1(JL,JK-1)
    ZDELP=PGEO1(JL,JK)-PGEO1(JL,JK-1)
    ZRHOHM1(JL,JK)=PAPHM1(JL,JK)*ZCONS1/ZTHM1(JL,JK)
    ZBVFHM1(JL,JK)=ZCONS2/ZTHM1(JL,JK)*&
!     & (1.0_JPRB-RCPD*ZRHOHM1(JL,JK)*(PTM1(JL,JK)-PTM1(JL,JK-1))/ZDELP)  
     & (1.0_JPRB+RCPD*(PTM1(JL,JK)-PTM1(JL,JK-1))/ZDELP)
    ZBVFHM1(JL,JK)=MAX(ZBVFHM1(JL,JK),GSSEC)
    ZBVFHM1(JL,JK)=SQRT(ZBVFHM1(JL,JK))
  ENDDO
ENDDO

!*       SET UP AZIMUTH DIRECTIONS AND SOME TRIG FACTORS
!*       -----------------------------------------------     

ZANG=2*RPI/IAZIDIM
ZAZ_FCT=1.0_JPRB

! get normalization factor to ensure that the same amount of momentum
! flux is directed (n,s,e,w) no mater how many azimuths are selected.
! note, however, the code below assumes a symmetric distribution of
! of azimuthal directions (ie 4,8,16,32,...)

ZNORM=0.0_JPRB
DO IAZI=1,IAZIDIM
  ZANG1=(IAZI-1)*ZANG
  ZCOSANG(IAZI)=COS(ZANG1)
  ZSINANG(IAZI)=SIN(ZANG1)
  ZNORM=ZNORM+ABS(ZCOSANG(IAZI))
ENDDO
ZAZ_FCT=2._JPRB*ZAZ_FCT/ZNORM


!*       DEFINE COORDINATE TRANSFORM
!*       -----------------------------------------------     

! note that this is expresed in terms of the intrinsic phase speed
! at launch ci=c-u_o so that the transformation is identical at every
! launch site.
! See Eq. 28-30 of Scinocca 2003.
     
ZXMAX=1.0_JPRB/ZCIMIN
ZXMIN=1.0_JPRB/ZCIMAX

ZXRAN=ZXMAX-ZXMIN
ZDX=ZXRAN/REAL(INCDIM-1)
IF(LGACALC) ZGAM=(ZXMAX-ZXMIN)/LOG(ZXMAX/ZXMIN)
ZX1=ZXRAN/(EXP(ZXRAN/ZGAM)-1.0_JPRB)
ZX2=ZXMIN-ZX1

DO INC=1,INCDIM
   ZTX=REAL(INC-1)*ZDX+ZXMIN
   ZX(INC)=ZX1*EXP((ZTX-ZXMIN)/ZGAM)+ZX2                       !Eq. 29 of Scinocca 2003
   ZCI(INC)=1.0_JPRB/ZX(INC)                                   !Eq. 28 of Scinocca 2003
   ZDCI(INC)=ZCI(INC)**2*(ZX1/ZGAM)*EXP((ZTX-ZXMIN)/ZGAM)*ZDX  !Eq. 30 of Scinocca 2003
ENDDO


!*       DEFINE INTRINSIC VELOCITY (RELATIVE TO LAUNCH LEVEL VELOCITY) U(Z)-U(Zo), AND COEFFICINETS
!*       ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------        

DO IAZI=1,IAZIDIM
   DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
      ZUL(JL,IAZI)=ZCOSANG(IAZI)*ZUHM1(JL,ILAUNCH)+ZSINANG(IAZI)*ZVHM1(JL,ILAUNCH)
   ENDDO
ENDDO
DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
   ZBVFL(JL)=ZBVFHM1(JL,ILAUNCH)
ENDDO

DO JK=2,ILAUNCH
   DO IAZI=1,IAZIDIM
      DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
         ZU=ZCOSANG(IAZI)*ZUHM1(JL,JK)+ZSINANG(IAZI)*ZVHM1(JL,JK)
         ZUI(JL,JK,IAZI)=ZU-ZUL(JL,IAZI)
      ENDDO
   ENDDO
ENDDO

!*       DEFINE RHO(Zo)/N(Zo)
!*       ------------------- 
DO JK=2,ILAUNCH
   DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
      ZFCT(JL,JK)=ZRHOHM1(JL,JK)/ZBVFHM1(JL,JK)
   ENDDO
ENDDO

! Optionally set ZFCT at launch level using standard atmos values, to ensure saturation is
! independent of location
IF (LGINDL) THEN
  ZCONS1=1.0_JPRB/RD
  ZCONS2=RG**2/RCPD
  ZDELP=STPHI(ILAUNCH)-STPHI(ILAUNCH-1)
  ZTHSTD=0.5_JPRB*(STTEM(ILAUNCH-1)+STTEM(ILAUNCH)) 
  ZRHOSTD=STPREH(ILAUNCH-1)*ZCONS1/ZTHSTD
  ZBVFSTD=ZCONS2/ZTHSTD*(1.0_JPRB+RCPD*(STTEM(ILAUNCH)-STTEM(ILAUNCH-1))/ZDELP)
  ZBVFSTD=MAX(ZBVFSTD,GSSEC)
  ZBVFSTD=SQRT(ZBVFSTD)
  DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
     ZFCT(JL,ILAUNCH)=ZRHOSTD/ZBVFSTD
  ENDDO
ENDIF

!*       SET LAUNCH MOMENTUM FLUX SPECTRAL DENSITY
!*       ----------------------------------------- 

! Eq. (25) of Scinocca 2003 (not including the 'A' component), and with U-Uo=0
! do this for only one azimuth since it is identical to all azimuths, and it will be renormalized
! Initial spectrum fully saturated if LGSATL

IF(NSLOPE==1) THEN
! s=1 case
   DO INC=1,INCDIM
      ZCIN=ZCI(INC)
      ZCIN4=(ZMS*ZCIN)**4
      DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
         ZBVFL4=ZBVFL(JL)**4
         IF(LGSATL) THEN
           ZFLUX(JL,INC,1)=ZFCT(JL,ILAUNCH)*ZBVFL4*MIN(ZCIN/ZCIN4,ZCIN/ZBVFL4)
         ELSE
           ZFLUX(JL,INC,1)=ZFCT(JL,ILAUNCH)*ZBVFL4*ZCIN/(ZBVFL4+ZCIN4)
         ENDIF
         ZACT(JL,INC,1)=1.0_JPRB
      ENDDO
   ENDDO
ELSEIF(NSLOPE==2) THEN
! s=2 case
   DO INC=1,INCDIM
      ZCIN=ZCI(INC)
      ZCIN4=(ZMS*ZCIN)**4
      DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
         ZBVFL4=ZBVFL(JL)**4
         ZCPEAK=ZBVFL(JL)/ZMS
         IF(LGSATL) THEN
           ZFLUX(JL,INC,1)=ZFCT(JL,ILAUNCH)*ZBVFL4*MIN(ZCPEAK/ZCIN4,ZCIN/ZBVFL4)
         ELSE
           ZFLUX(JL,INC,1)=ZFCT(JL,ILAUNCH)*ZBVFL4*ZCIN*ZCPEAK/(ZBVFL4*ZCPEAK+ZCIN4*ZCIN)
         ENDIF
         ZACT(JL,INC,1)=1.0_JPRB
      ENDDO
   ENDDO
ELSEIF(NSLOPE==-1) THEN
! s=-1 case
  DO INC=1,INCDIM
     ZCIN=ZCI(INC)
     ZCIN2=(ZMS*ZCIN)**2
     DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
        ZBVFL2=ZBVFL(JL)**2
        ZFLUX(JL,INC,1)=ZFCT(JL,ILAUNCH)*ZBVFL2*ZCIN/(ZBVFL2+ZCIN2)
        ZACT(JL,INC,1)=1.0_JPRB
     ENDDO
  ENDDO
ELSEIF(NSLOPE==0) THEN
! s=0 case
  DO INC=1,INCDIM
     ZCIN=ZCI(INC)
     ZCIN3=(ZMS*ZCIN)**3
     DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
        ZBVFL3=ZBVFL(JL)**3
        ZFLUX(JL,INC,1)=ZFCT(JL,ILAUNCH)*ZBVFL3*ZCIN/(ZBVFL3+ZCIN3)
        ZACT(JL,INC,1)=1.0_JPRB
        ZACC(JL,INC,1)=1.0_JPRB
     ENDDO
  ENDDO
ENDIF

!*       NORMALIZE LAUNCH MOMENTUM FLUX
!*       ------------------------------

! (rho x F^H = rho_o x F_p^total)

! integrate (ZFLUX x dX)
DO INC=1,INCDIM
   ZCINC=ZDCI(INC)
   DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
      ZPU(JL,ILAUNCH,1)=ZPU(JL,ILAUNCH,1)+ZFLUX(JL,INC,1)*ZCINC
   ENDDO
ENDDO

!*       NORMALIZE GFLUXLAUN TO INCLUDE SENSITIVITY TO PRECIPITATION
!*       -----------------------------------------------------------

! Also other options to alter tropical values

! A=ZFNORM in Scinocca 2003.  A is independent of height.
ZDXA=1.0_JPRB/29.E3_JPRB
ZDXB=1.0_JPRB/3.5E3_JPRB
DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA       
  ZDX=MAX(1.E2_JPRB,2*RA*SQRT(RPI*PGAW(JL))) !grid resolution (m)
   !Scaling factor for launch flux depending on grid resolution
   ! smooth reduction below 30 km
  ZDXS=1.0_JPRB-MIN(1.0_JPRB,ATAN((MAX(1.0_JPRB/ZDX,ZDXA)-ZDXA)/(ZDXB-ZDXA)))
  ZFLUXLAUN(JL)=ZFLUXGLOB*ZDXS
  ZFNORM(JL)=ZFLUXLAUN(JL)/ZPU(JL,ILAUNCH,1)
ENDDO

! If LOZPR=TRUR then vary EPLAUNCH over tropics      
IF (LOZPR) THEN
  IF (NGAUSS==1) THEN
    DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA       
       ZFLUXLAUN(JL)=ZFLUXLAUN(JL)*(1.0_JPRB+MIN(0.5_JPRB,GCOEFF*PPRECIP(JL)))     !precip
       ZFNORM(JL)=ZFLUXLAUN(JL)/ZPU(JL,ILAUNCH,1)
    ENDDO
  ELSEIF (NGAUSS==2) THEN           
    DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA       
      ZGELATDEG=PGELAT(JL)*ZRADTODEG 
      ZGAUSS(JL)=ZGAUSSB*EXP((-ZGELATDEG*ZGELATDEG)/(2*GGAUSSA*GGAUSSA))
      ZFLUXLAUN(JL)=(1.0_JPRB+ZGAUSS(JL))*ZFLUXLAUN(JL)
      ZFNORM(JL)=ZFLUXLAUN(JL)/ZPU(JL,ILAUNCH,1)
    ENDDO
  ELSEIF (NGAUSS==4) THEN           
! Set latitudinal dependence to optimize stratospheric winds for 36r1
    Z50S=-50.0_JPRB
    DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA       
      ZGELATDEG=PGELAT(JL)*ZRADTODEG-Z50S
      ZGAUSS(JL)=ZGAUSSB*EXP((-ZGELATDEG*ZGELATDEG)/(2*GGAUSSA*GGAUSSA))
      ZFLUXLAUN(JL)=(1.0_JPRB+ZGAUSS(JL))*ZFLUXLAUN(JL)
      ZFNORM(JL)=ZFLUXLAUN(JL)/ZPU(JL,ILAUNCH,1)
    ENDDO
  ENDIF
ENDIF 
 
DO IAZI=1,IAZIDIM
   DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
      ZPU(JL,ILAUNCH,IAZI)=ZFLUXLAUN(JL)
   ENDDO
ENDDO

!*       ADJUST CONSTANT ZFCT
!*       --------------------
DO JK=2,ILAUNCH
   DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
      ZFCT(JL,JK)=ZFNORM(JL)*ZFCT(JL,JK)
   ENDDO
ENDDO

!*       RENORMALIZE EACH SPECTRAL ELEMENT IN FIRST AZIMUTH
!*       --------------------------------------------------
DO INC=1,INCDIM
   DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
      ZFLUX(JL,INC,1)=ZFNORM(JL)*ZFLUX(JL,INC,1)
   ENDDO
ENDDO

!*       COPY ZFLUX INTO ALL OTHER AZIMUTHS
!*       --------------------------------

! ZACT=1 then no critical level
! ZACT=0 then critical level

DO IAZI=2,IAZIDIM
   DO INC=1,INCDIM
      DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
         ZFLUX(JL,INC,IAZI)=ZFLUX(JL,INC,1)
         ZACT(JL,INC,IAZI)=1.0_JPRB
         ZACC(JL,INC,IAZI)=1.0_JPRB
      ENDDO
   ENDDO
ENDDO

! -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

!*       BEGIN MAIN LOOP OVER LEVELS
!*       ---------------------------

!* begin IAZIDIM do-loop
!* --------------------

DO IAZI=1,IAZIDIM

!* begin JK do-loop
!* ----------------

   DO JK=ILAUNCH-1,2,-1


!* first do critical levels
!* ------------------------

         DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
            ZCI_MIN(JL,IAZI)=MAX(ZCI_MIN(JL,IAZI),ZUI(JL,JK,IAZI))               
         ENDDO

!* set ZACT to zero if critical level encountered
!* ----------------------------------------------

         Z0P5=0.5_JPRB
         DO INC=1,INCDIM
            ZCIN=ZCI(INC)
            DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
               ZATMP=Z0P5+SIGN(Z0P5,ZCIN-ZCI_MIN(JL,IAZI))
               ZACC(JL,INC,IAZI)=ZACT(JL,INC,IAZI)-ZATMP
               ZACT(JL,INC,IAZI)=ZATMP
            ENDDO
         ENDDO

!* integrate to get critical-level contribution to mom deposition on this level, i.e. ZACC=1
!* ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

         DO INC=1,INCDIM
            ZCINC=ZDCI(INC)
            DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
               ZDFL(JL,JK,IAZI)=ZDFL(JL,JK,IAZI)+&
     &                ZACC(JL,INC,IAZI)*ZFLUX(JL,INC,IAZI)*ZCINC
            ENDDO
         ENDDO

!* get weighted average of phase speed in layer
!* --------------------------------------------

         DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
            IF(ZDFL(JL,JK,IAZI)>0.0_JPRB) THEN
               ZATMP=ZCRT(JL,JK,IAZI)
               DO INC=1,INCDIM
                  ZATMP=ZATMP+ZCI(INC)*&
     &                   ZACC(JL,INC,IAZI)*ZFLUX(JL,INC,IAZI)*ZDCI(INC)
               ENDDO
               ZCRT(JL,JK,IAZI)=ZATMP/ZDFL(JL,JK,IAZI)
            ELSE
               ZCRT(JL,JK,IAZI)=ZCRT(JL,JK+1,IAZI)
            ENDIF
         ENDDO

!* do saturation (Eq. (26) and (27) of Scinocca 2003)
!* -------------------------------------------------

         IF(GPTWO==3.0_JPRB) THEN
             DO INC=1,INCDIM
                ZCIN=ZCI(INC)
                ZCINC=1.0_JPRB/ZCIN
                DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
                   ZE1=ZCIN-ZUI(JL,JK,IAZI)
                   ZE2=GCSTAR*ZFCT(JL,JK)*ZE1
                   ZFLUXSQ=ZE2*ZE2*ZE1*ZCINC
                !  ZFLUXSQ=ZE2*ZE2*ZE1/ZCIN
                   ZDEP=ZACT(JL,INC,IAZI)*(ZFLUX(JL,INC,IAZI)**2-ZFLUXSQ)
                   IF(ZDEP>0.0_JPRB) THEN
                      ZFLUX(JL,INC,IAZI)=SQRT(ZFLUXSQ)
                   ENDIF
                ENDDO
             ENDDO
         ELSEIF(GPTWO==2.0_JPRB) THEN
             DO INC=1,INCDIM
                ZCIN=ZCI(INC)
                ZCINC=1.0_JPRB/ZCIN
                DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
                   ZFLUXS=GCSTAR*ZFCT(JL,JK)*&
     &                    (ZCIN-ZUI(JL,JK,IAZI))**2*ZCINC
                !  ZFLUXS=GCSTAR*ZFCT(JL,JK)*(ZCIN-ZUI(JL,JK,IAZI))**2/ZCIN
                   ZDEP=ZACT(JL,INC,IAZI)*(ZFLUX(JL,INC,IAZI)-ZFLUXS)
                   IF(ZDEP>0.0_JPRB) THEN
                      ZFLUX(JL,INC,IAZI)=ZFLUXS
                   ENDIF
                ENDDO
             ENDDO
         ENDIF
            
!* integrate spectrum
!* ------------------            

         DO INC=1,INCDIM
            ZCINC=ZDCI(INC)
            DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
               ZPU(JL,JK,IAZI)=ZPU(JL,JK,IAZI)+&
     &               ZACT(JL,INC,IAZI)*ZFLUX(JL,INC,IAZI)*ZCINC
            ENDDO
         ENDDO

!* end JK do-loop
!* --------------

     ENDDO

!* end IAZIDIM do-loop
!* ---------------

ENDDO

! -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

!*       MAKE CORRECTION FOR CRITICAL-LEVEL MOMENTUM DEPOSITION
!*       ------------------------------------------------------

Z0P0=0._JPRB
ZRGPTS=1.0_JPRB/(RG*PTSTEP)
DO IAZI=1,IAZIDIM
   DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
     ZCNGL(JL)=0.0_JPRB
   ENDDO
   DO JK=2,ILAUNCH
      DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
         ZULM=ZCOSANG(IAZI)*PUM1(JL,JK)+ZSINANG(IAZI)*PVM1(JL,JK)-ZUL(JL,IAZI)
         ZDFL(JL,JK-1,IAZI)=ZDFL(JL,JK-1,IAZI)+ZCNGL(JL)
         ZDFT=MIN(ZDFL(JL,JK-1,IAZI),2.0_JPRB*(PAPM1(JL,JK-1)-PAPM1(JL,JK))*&
&                 (ZCRT(JL,JK-1,IAZI)-ZULM)*ZRGPTS)
         ZDFT=MAX(ZDFT,Z0P0)
         ZCNGL(JL)=(ZDFL(JL,JK-1,IAZI)-ZDFT)
         ZPU(JL,JK,IAZI)=ZPU(JL,JK,IAZI)-ZCNGL(JL)
       ENDDO
   ENDDO
ENDDO


!*       SUM CONTRIBUTION FOR TOTAL ZONAL AND MERIDIONAL FLUX
!*       ---------------------------------------------------

DO IAZI=1,IAZIDIM
   DO JK=ILAUNCH,2,-1
      DO JL=KIDIA,KFDIA
         PFLUXU(JL,JK)=PFLUXU(JL,JK)+ZPU(JL,JK,IAZI)*ZAZ_FCT*ZCOSANG(IAZI)
         PFLUXV(JL,JK)=PFLUXV(JL,JK)+ZPU(JL,JK,IAZI)*ZAZ_FCT*ZSINANG(IAZI)
      ENDDO
   ENDDO
ENDDO


!*    UPDATE U AND V TENDENCIES
!*    ----------------------------   

ZCONS1=1.0_JPRB/RCPD
DO JK=1,ILAUNCH
   DO JL=KIDIA, KFDIA
      ZDELP= RG/(PAPHM1(JL,JK+1)-PAPHM1(JL,JK))  
      ZE1=(PFLUXU(JL,JK+1)-PFLUXU(JL,JK))*ZDELP
      ZE2=(PFLUXV(JL,JK+1)-PFLUXV(JL,JK))*ZDELP   
      PTENU(JL,JK)=ZE1
      PTENV(JL,JK)=ZE2
   ENDDO
ENDDO

!--------------------------------------------------------------------------- 

END ASSOCIATE
IF (LHOOK) CALL DR_HOOK('GWDRAG_WMS',1,ZHOOK_HANDLE)

END SUBROUTINE GWDRAG_WMS

```

The frequency dependent part is given by\n\n(3)\n\nThe normalization factorsandensure thatand. It should be noted that any other spectral shape could be assumed here. The nominal values ofare chosen (Quinn et al.\n, 2020 ; Warner & McIntyre, 1996 ) since the coefficients vary only by a factor of two for plausible departures from these values (Fritts & VanZandt, 1993 ). Note that the energy amplitudeis a density in wave directionand upon integrating over appropriate direction ranges, normalization must also be applied by dividing the integrand by.\n\nThis spectrum in Equation 1 is inserted into the energy balance equation,\n\n(4)\n\nwhich is equivalent to wave action conservation (Bretherton & Garrett, 1968 ) for waves in the presence of a vertically sheared background flow.\n\nThe terms on the left-hand side of Equation 4 are the time variation of the wavefield amplitude and the corresponding group velocities in,andspaces, that is,andrespectively, which are defined below. The first term on the right-hand side is the interaction term between the wavefield and the mean flow. Theterm denotes sources and sinks and other non-linear effects.\n\n...\n\nIDEMIX is implemented into the UA-ICON model by means of a new module to the main ICON code. It is validated against the current standard WMS NOGWP and also the cutting-edge transient MS-GWaM model, using the same columnar setup as Bölöni et al. ( 2021 ). For MS-GWaM results presented here, a slightly older version of UA-ICON was used compared to the IDEMIX and WMS simulations. A grid with horizontal spacing of approximately 160 km (R2B04), 120 vertical layers to a grid height of 150 km is employed. The ICON simulations are initialized with operational Integrated Forecasting System (IFS) of the ECMWF analyses for 1 May or 1 November 1991. The data is extrapolated to the model top. Two month simulations are performed with results showing the field averaged over the month of June or December, thus discarding the time for the mean field to adjust.\n\n...\n\nIn June, IDEMIX shows also mainly positive GWD in both hemispheres, similar to MS-GWaM and WMS, although IDEMIX has a much greater magnitude of meridional GWD. Conversely, in December, whereas MS-GWaM and WMS show mainly negative GWD, IDEMIX exhibits a positive GWD in the northern hemisphere and also at low southern latitudes in Figure 8e .\n\n **Figure 8**\n\nOpen in figure viewer [PowerPoint](https://agupubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/action/downloadFigures?id=jame70070-fig-0008&partId=&doi=10.1029%2F2023MS004121)\n\nMeridional gravity wave drag (m/s/day) in (a)–(c) June 1991 and (d)–(f) December 1991. Forcing flux,,andkm.\n\n### 3\\.3 Effects of Wave Forcing\n\nFigure 9i shows that by using a reduced wave forcingin IDEMIX, in December there is improved closure of the winter jet around 100 km. Note that for numerical stability, the valuehas now been chosen.\n\n...\n\nThe one-dimensional wave ray-tracing MS-GWaM is about five times slower than WMS.\n\nNoteworthy with IDEMIX, is the reversal of the zonal GWD around the mesopause region. This GWD reversal, not simulated by WMS or MS-GWaM, is observed in the high resolution GW-resolving simulations presented by Becker and Vadas ( 2018 ), where they hypothesize that this is due to secondary gravity wave emission after primary wave breakdown. It is also observed in the Middle and Upper Atmosphere Model (Lilienthal et al., 2020 ) with a nonlinear spectral whole atmosphere GWP (Yiǧit et al., 2008 ) for January conditions where it is suggested that the faster GWs are necessary to influence circulation patterns and temperature differences in the lower thermosphere, such as the layered meridional wind structures, observed here also with IDEMIX for smaller forcing magnitudes.\nHere, it is suggested that this GWD reversal will only be present when there is a significant in wave momentum flux at these higher altitudes, whether that comes from the further-traveling high-phase speed, smaller amplitude primary waves' interaction with the mean circulation or from secondary gravity wave sources.\n\nIDEMIX has the potential to include multiple wave sources simply by defining a forcing flux, at multiple altitudes. It can be further developed to include lateral wave propagation by removing the single-column assumption. Then the energy balance equation will also have lateral group velocities, similar to the second and third terms in Equation 4 but in theanddirections.\n\n### Acronyms\n\n|- IDEMIX |- Internal wave Dissipation, Energy and Mixing |\n| --- | --- |\n|- GW |- Gravity wave |\n|- GWP |- Gravity wave parameterization |\n|- NOGWP |- Nonorographic gravity wave parameterization |\n|- MS-GWaM |- Multiscale gravity wave model |\n|- ICON |- Icosahedral nonhydrostatic |\n|- UA-ICON |- Upper atmosphere icosahedral nonhydrostatic |\n|- ECMWF |- European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts |\n|- GWD |- Gravity wave drag |\n|- WMS |- Warner McIntyre Scinocca |\n|- UARS |- Upper Atmosphere Research Satellite |\n|- URAP |- UARS Reference Atmosphere Project |\n|- HWM |- Horizontal wind model |\n\n## Acknowledgments\n\nThis paper is a contribution to the subproject W1 (Gravity wave parameterization for the atmosphere) of the Collaborative Research Centre TRR 181 “Energy Transfer in Atmosphere and Ocean” funded by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG; German Research Foundation)—Project 274762653. Ulrich Achatz thanks the DFG for partial support through CRC 301 “TPChange” (Project-ID 428312742, Projects B06 “Impact of small-scale dynamics on UTLS transport and mixing” and B07 “Impact of cirrus clouds on tropopause structure”).\n\n...\n\n1029%2F2023MS004121&doiOfLink=10.1175%2FJPO-D-16-0056.1&linkType=gs&linkLocation=Reference&linkSource=FULL_TEXT)\n* Ern, M. , Preusse, P. , & Warner, C. D. ( 2006 ).         "REF2014\n\n* About\n* How to search\n* FAQs\n* API\n* Terms of Use\n* REF2014 Home\n\n### Gravity-wave parametrization in weather forecast and climate models\n\n#### **Submitting Institution**\n\nUniversity of Cambridge\n\n#### **Unit of Assessment**\n\nMathematical Sciences\n\n#### **Summary Impact Type**\n\nTechnological\n\n#### **Research Subject Area(s)**\n\nMathematical Sciences: Pure Mathematics  \nEarth Sciences: Oceanography  \nEngineering: Maritime Engineering\n\n  \n#### **Download original**\n\n[PDF](https://ref2014impact.azurewebsites.net/casestudies2/refservice.svc/GetCaseStudyPDF/17638)\n\n[View similar case studies](https://impact.ref.ac.uk/casestudies/Results.aspx?Id=17638)\n\n### **Summary of the impact**\n\nThe Warner-McIntyre parametrization scheme for non-topographic\natmospheric gravity waves,\ndeveloped at the Department of Applied Mathematics and Theoretical Physics\n(DAMTP),\nUniversity of Cambridge, during the period from 1993 to 2004, has since\n2010 been used by the\n\n...\n\ngravity waves are generated by processes such as thunderstorms.) The\npotential role of non-topographic\ngravity waves in determining aspects of the large-scale atmospheric\ncirculation,\nparticularly in the stratosphere and mesosphere, and consequently the need\nto represent such\nwaves in numerical models was recognised in the 1980s and 1990s. In 1993\nresearchers at the\nUniversity of Cambridge Department of Applied Mathematics and Theoretical\nPhysics (DAMTP),\nC.D. Warner (DAMTP Research Associate from 1992, Senior Research Associate\nfrom 2003) and\nM.E. McIntyre (DAMTP Professor throughout the period), began work on the\nformulation of a\nparametrization scheme in which the propagation and dissipation of a field\nof waves made up of a\nbroad spectrum of frequencies was calculated by following individual\nspectral elements [Ref 1].\nCompared to other schemes existing at that time, this scheme (the `full'\nscheme) gave the\nadvantage of physical and mathematical clarity, but was too\ncomputationally expensive to be\nuseable in numerical models of the large-scale atmosphere. In following\nwork [Refs 2 and 4] an\n`ultra-simple' version of the scheme, that required only a small number of\nvariables to characterize\nthe gravity wave field as a function of height and time at each horizontal\nlocation, was developed\nand validated by Warner and McIntyre by testing against their full scheme.\nAlongside this work\ndeveloping a robust and properly formulated gravity-wave scheme there was\na collaborative\nprogramme of work with the UK Met Office to establish, as new versions of\nthe gravity-wave\nscheme became available, the effect on the circulation as represented by a\nglobal-scale model.\n(The equations describing the parametrization were added to the large\ncomputer code defining the\nmodel.) The global model was a version, at that time used for research\npurposes only, of the Met\nOffice Unified Model, `vertically extended' to include a detailed\nrepresentation of the stratosphere.\nResults showing the effect of the gravity-wave scheme on the modelled\ncirculation were reported in\nRefs 3, 5 and 6.\n\n### **References to the research**\n\n1\\. \\*Warner, CD; McIntyre, ME, 1996: On the propagation and dissipation of\ngravity wave\nspectra through a realistic middle atmosphere. J. Atmos. Sci., 53,\n3213-3235. DOI:\n10\\.1175/1520-0469(1996)053<3213:OTPADO>2.0.CO;2\n\n[](http://gateway.webofknowledge.com/gateway/Gateway.cgi?GWVersion=2&SrcApp=DigitalScience&SrcAuth=CEL_HEFCE&KeyUT=WOS:A1996VV30400003&DestLinkType=FullRecord&DestApp=WOS_CPL&UsrCustomerID=cdf33fbcc558d8a03f830789302d37db)\n\n2\\. Warner, CD; McIntyre, ME, 1999: Toward an ultra-simple spectral\ngravity wave\nparameterization for general circulation models. (Proceeding of\nInternational Symposium on\nDynamics and Structure of the Mesopause Region, Kyoto Univ., Kyoto, Japan,\nMarch 16-21\n1998\\. Earth, Planets and Space, 51, 7-8. 475-484.\n\n3\\. Scaife, AA, Butchart, N, Warner, CD, Stainforth, D., Norton, WA,\nAustin, J.\n, 2000: Realistic\nquasi-biennial oscillations in a simulation of the global climate.\nGeophys. Res. Lett., 27,\n3481-3484. DOI: 10.1029/2000GL011625.\n\n[](http://gateway.webofknowledge.com/gateway/Gateway.cgi?GWVersion=2&SrcApp=DigitalScience&SrcAuth=CEL_HEFCE&KeyUT=WOS:000165063000012&DestLinkType=FullRecord&DestApp=WOS_CPL&UsrCustomerID=cdf33fbcc558d8a03f830789302d37db) [](http://www.scopus.com/inward/record.url?partnerID=HzOxMe3b&eid=2-s2.0-0034352509&origin=inward)\n\n4\\. \\*Warner, CD, McIntyre, ME, 2001: An ultrasimple spectral\nparameterization for\nnonorographic gravity waves. J. Atmos. Sci., 58, 1837-1857. DOI:\n10\\.1175/1520-0469(2001)058<1837:AUSPFN>2.0.CO;2\n\n[](http://gateway.webofknowledge.com/gateway/Gateway.cgi?GWVersion=2&SrcApp=DigitalScience&SrcAuth=CEL_HEFCE&KeyUT=WOS:000169762500001&DestLinkType=FullRecord&DestApp=WOS_CPL&UsrCustomerID=cdf33fbcc558d8a03f830789302d37db)\n\n5\\. \\*Scaife AA, Butchart, N, Warner, CD, Swinbank, RC, 2002: Impact of a\nspectral gravity\nwave parameterization on the stratosphere in the met office unified model.\nJ. ## Assistant (Plan · DeepSeek V4 Flash Free · 7.8s)

**WMS = Warner–McIntyre–Scinocca.** 

`gwdrag_wms` is the ECMWF IFS non-orographic gravity wave drag scheme (ported into ICON as `mo_gwd_wms`, called for `inwp_gwd=1`). The WMS acronym stands for the two schemes it derives from:

- **Warner and McIntyre (1996)** — the original ultra-simple spectral non-orographic GW parameterization (full hydrostatic/non-rotational version too costly for operations)
- **Scinocca (2003)** with **McLandress and Scinocca (2005)** — the hydrostatic, non-rotational simplified version used in operations

This is confirmed by the header in mo_gwd_wms.f90:15-19 ("After Scinocca (2003) and McLandress and Scinoccia (JAS 2005), hydrostatic non-rotational simplified version of the Warner and McIntyre (1996)... parameterization") and the reference at line 21-23 (Orr et al. 2010, the paper that implemented it into IFS). The literature explicitly documents the "WMS" designation (e.g. the JAMES 2025 IDEMIX paper's acronym table lists WMS = "Warner McIntyre Scinocca").

```
3004:     INTEGER             ,INTENT(IN)    :: jstep     ! number of current (large) time step
3005:                                                     ! performed in current domain
3006:     INTEGER             ,INTENT(IN)    :: iau_iter  ! counter for IAU iteration
3007:     REAL(wp)            ,INTENT(IN)    :: dt_phy    ! physics time step for current patch
3008: 
3009:     TYPE(datetime)      ,INTENT(IN)    :: mtime_current
3010: 
3011:     CHARACTER(len=*), PARAMETER :: routine = modname//':perform_dyn_substepping'
3012: 
3013:     ! local variables
3014:     INTEGER                  :: jg                ! domain ID
3015:     INTEGER                  :: nstep             ! timestep counter
3016:     INTEGER                  :: ndyn_substeps_tot ! total number of dynamics substeps
3017:                                                   ! since last boundary update
3018:     REAL(wp)                 :: dt_dyn            ! dynamics time step
3019:     REAL(wp)                 :: cur_time          ! current time (for IAU)
3020: 
3021:     LOGICAL                  :: lclean_mflx       ! .TRUE.: first substep
3022:     LOGICAL                  :: l_recompute       ! .TRUE.: recompute velocity tendencies for predictor
3023:                                                   ! (first substep)
3024:     LOGICAL                  :: lsave_mflx
3025:     LOGICAL                  :: lprep_adv         !.TRUE.: do computations for preparing tracer advection in solve_nh
3026:     LOGICAL                  :: llast             !.TRUE.: this is the last substep
3027:     TYPE(timeDelta)          :: time_diff
3028:     !-------------------------------------------------------------------------
3029: 
3030:     ! get domain ID
3031:     jg = p_patch%id
3032: 
3033:     ! compute dynamics timestep
3034:     dt_dyn = dt_phy/ndyn_substeps_var(jg)
3035: 
3036:     IF ( ltransport .OR. p_patch%n_childdom > 0 .AND. grf_intmethod_e == 6 ) THEN
3037:       lprep_adv = .TRUE. ! do computations for preparing tracer advection in solve_nh
3038:     ELSE
3039:       lprep_adv = .FALSE.
3040:     ENDIF
3041: 
3042:     ! airmass_now
3043:     CALL compute_airmass(p_patch   = p_patch,                       & !in
3044:       &                  p_metrics = p_nh_state%metrics,            & !in
3045:       &                  rho       = p_nh_state%prog(nnow(jg))%rho, & !in
3046:       &                  airmass   = p_nh_state%diag%airmass_now,   & !inout
3047:       &                  lacc      = .TRUE.)                          !in
3048: 
3049:     ! perform dynamics substepping
3050:     !
3051:     SUBSTEPS: DO nstep = 1, ndyn_substeps_var(jg)
3052: 
3053:       ! Print control output for maximum horizontal and vertical wind speed
3054:       !
3055:       ! 3 Cases:
3056:       ! msg_level E [12, inf[: print max/min output for every domain and every substep
3057:       ! msg_level E [ 8,  11]: print max/min output for global domain and every substep
3058:       ! msg_level E [ 5,   7]: print max/min output for global domain and first substep
3059:       !
3060:       IF (msg_level >= 12 &
3061:         & .OR. msg_level >= 8 .AND. jg == 1 &
3062:         & .OR. msg_level >= 5 .AND. jg == 1 .AND. nstep == 1) THEN
3063:         CALL print_maxwinds(p_patch, p_nh_state%prog(nnow(jg))%vn,   &
3064:           p_nh_state%prog(nnow(jg))%w, lacc=.TRUE.)
3065:       ENDIF
3066: 
3067:       ! total number of dynamics substeps since last boundary update
3068:       ! applicable to refined domains only
3069:       ndyn_substeps_tot = (jstep-1)*ndyn_substeps_var(jg) + nstep
3070: 
3071:       ! nullify prep_adv fields at first substep
3072:       lclean_mflx = (nstep==1)
3073:       l_recompute = lclean_mflx
3074: 
3075:       ! logical checking for the last substep
3076:       llast = (nstep==ndyn_substeps_var(jg))
3077: 
3078:       ! save massflux at first substep
3079:       lsave_mflx = (p_patch%n_childdom > 0 .AND. nstep == 1 )
3080: 
3081:       IF (init_mode == MODE_IAU) THEN ! incremental analysis mode
3082:         time_diff  =  getTimeDeltaFromDateTime(mtime_current, time_config%tc_exp_startdate)
3083:         cur_time = REAL(getTotalSecondsTimedelta(time_diff, mtime_current)                  &
3084:              &         -getTotalSecondsTimedelta(time_config%timeshift%mtime_shift, mtime_current),wp)  &
3085:              &    +(REAL(nstep-ndyn_substeps_var(jg),wp)-0.5_wp)*dt_dyn
3086:         IF (iau_iter == 1) THEN
3087:           CALL compute_iau_wgt(cur_time, dt_dyn, 0.5_wp*dt_iau, lclean_mflx)
3088:         ELSE
3089:           CALL compute_iau_wgt(cur_time, dt_dyn, dt_iau, lclean_mflx)
3090:         ENDIF
3091:       ENDIF
3092: 
3093:       ! integrate dynamical core
3094:       CALL solve_nh(p_nh_state, p_patch, p_int_state, prep_adv,     &
3095:         &           nnow(jg), nnew(jg), linit_dyn(jg), l_recompute, &
3096:         &           lsave_mflx, lprep_adv, lclean_mflx, nstep,      &
3097:         &           ndyn_substeps_tot-1, dt_dyn, lacc=.TRUE.)
3098: 
3099:       ! now reset linit_dyn to .FALSE.
3100:       linit_dyn(jg) = .FALSE.
3101: 
3102: 
3103:       ! Finally, switch between time levels now and new for next iteration
3104:       !
3105:       ! Note, that we do not swap during the very last iteration.
3106:       ! This final swap is postponed till the end of the integration step.
3107:       IF ( .NOT. llast ) THEN
3108:         CALL swap(nnow(jg), nnew(jg))
3109:       ENDIF
3110: 
3111:     END DO SUBSTEPS
3112: 
3113:     IF (init_mode == MODE_IAU) THEN
3114:       IF (cur_time > dt_iau) lready_for_checkpoint = .TRUE.
3115:     ELSE
3116:       lready_for_checkpoint = .TRUE.
3117:     ENDIF
3118: 
3119:     ! airmass_new
3120:     CALL compute_airmass(p_patch   = p_patch,                       & !in
3121:       &                  p_metrics = p_nh_state%metrics,            & !in
3122:       &                  rho       = p_nh_state%prog(nnew(jg))%rho, & !in
3123:       &                  airmass   = p_nh_state%diag%airmass_new,   & !inout
3124:       &                  lacc      = .TRUE.)                          !in
3125: 
3126:     IF (nlev_hcfl(jg) > 0) THEN
3127:       CALL compute_hcfl(p_patch, p_nh_state%prog(nnew(jg))%vn, dt_dyn, nlev_hcfl(jg), p_nh_state%diag%max_hcfl_dyn)
3128:     ENDIF
3129: 
3130:   END SUBROUTINE perform_dyn_substepping
3131: 
3132: 
3133:   !-------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

Actually, importantly: In ICON, dynamics uses vn as prognostic. u and v are diagnostic, recovered from vn via RBF interpolation. The physics operates on u,v at cell centers, producing ddt_u_gwd etc. These get interpolated to vn-space (ddt_vn_phy) for use in the vn equation. So the "zonal wind u(t+dt)" is a diagnosed quantity obtained from the updated vn.

The AD boundary: to differentiate u(t+dt) w.r.t. gfluxlaun, you'd need to trace through: gfluxlaun → gwdrag_wms → ddt_u_gwd → z_ddt_u_tot → interpolate → ddt_vn_phy → solve_nh vn update → rbf_vec_interpol_cell → u(t+dt).

Complications:
1. gwdrag_wms is called only every dt_gwd (usually a multiple of fast-physics dt), NOT every time step. So the tendency is held constant (persisted in prm_nwp_tend) and applied over multiple steps. This is a "physics called at reduced frequency" complication.
2. In-place accumulation: ddt_u_gwd is used as ptenu output of gwdrag_wms then modified in-place (0.125 scaling at lowest level, SIGN-limiting, SPPT perturbation).
3. prm_nwp_tend is module state (persistent across timesteps) - container type with deferred allocation. So AD needs to track it as persistent state.
4. The interpolation from cell (u,v) to edge (vn) is nontrivial linear interpolation — differentiable but needs care.
5. Multiple physics tendencies summed into z_ddt_u_tot means ∂(vn tendency)/∂gfluxlaun only goes through ddt_u_gwd component; but the sum is fine.
6. solve_nh has 2 substeps (predictor-corrector), dtime scaling, time weighting of the advection terms; the physics tendency enters in both predictor and corrector, so vn(nnew) = vn(nnow) + dtime*(... + ddt_vn_phy) in both steps.

8. Turbulence is applied directly to vn (fast physics) within nwp_nh_interface, which modifies pt_prog%vn, and pt_diag%u — this is a state update in-place.

Key complications for AD:
- **Variable space change**: physics in u/v (cell), dynamics in vn (edge). Two linear interpolation operations (c_lin_e and rbf_vec_interpol_cell) that are themselves AD-relevant.
- **One-step time lag**: physics tendencies used in dynamics are from previous physics call (slow physics). So ∂u/∂param passes through a time-lagged state — meaning full rollout differentiation must traverse many time steps; the tendency saved is applied one step later.
- **In-place accumulation/modification**: tendencies are added into prm_nwp_tend (module state, persistent across steps, from t_nwp_phy_tend). `prm_nwp_tend` is a persistent (module-global) variable in t_phy_state? - **Substepping**: solve_nh is called ndyn_substeps_var times per physics step with dt_dyn = dt_phy/ndyn_substeps.

## The code path (physics tendency → zonal wind update)

**Key architectural fact up front:** ICON's *prognostic* horizontal wind is **not** `u`. It is `vn`, the edge-normal velocity component (mo_atm_dyn_iconam/mo_nonhydro_types.f90:155-166). `u`/`v` are *diagnostic* zonal/meridional winds reconstructed from `vn` by interpolation (`rbf_vec_interpol_cell`, mo_nh_stepping.f90:3301). So the physics→wind chain passes through a **cell→edge→cell** round trip: physics works on `u,v` at cell centers, the tendency is projected onto edges (`ddt_vn_phy`), the dynamics integrates `vn`, and `u` is then re-diagnosed from `vn`.

### Step 1 — Physics schemes produce zonal tendencies at cell centers

| Scheme | Routine (file) | Output → where | line |
|---|---|---|---|
| **GWD** (WMS) | `gwdrag_wms` (src/atm_phy_schemes/mo_gwd_wms.f90) | `ptenu` = zonal momentum tendency | :57 (subroutine), :57 sig, ptenu declared ~:57 |
| GWD driver | `nwp_gwdrag` (src/atm_phy_nwp/mo_nwp_gw_interface.f90) | `ptenu → prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_gwd` | :256 (`ptenu = prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_gwd`) |
| SSO drag | `nwp_gwdrag` (same routine) | `prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_sso` | :181 |
| Turbulence | `nwp_turbdiff` (src/atm_phy_nwp/mo_nwp_turbdiff_interface.f90) | `prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_turb` | :641/a:716 |
| Convection | `nwp_convection` (mo_nwp_conv_interface.f90) | `prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_pconv` | — |
| Radiative heating | `nwp_radiation` | `ddt_temp_radsw/lw` (thermal only) | — |
| Rayleigh friction | inside `nwp_nh_interface` | local `zddt_u_raylfric` | :2431 |

All stored in the `t_nwp_phy_tend` container `prm_nwp_tend` (declared in mo_nwp_phy_types.f90:607-626). Note: **`prm_nwp_tend` is persistent module state** (allocated once, reused every step), and several schemes *modify-in-place* — e.g. GWD scaling/limiting at mo_nwp_gw_interface.f90:268-281 and SPPT perturbation at :306.

### Step 2 — Tendencies are accumulated

In `nwp_nh_interface` (src/atm_phy_nwp/mo_nh_interface_nwp.f90:2454-2457), the total **slow-physics** zonal tendency is a plain sum into local scratch arrays:

```
z_ddt_u_tot = ddt_u_gwd + zddt_u_raylfric + ddt_u_sso + ddt_u_pconv
z_ddt_v_tot = ddt_v_gwd + zddt_v_raylfric + ddt_v_sso + ddt_v_pconv
```

Turbulence is **not** in this sum — it is "fast physics", applied directly (see step 3).

### Step 3 — Total tendency is projected onto the dynamical variable

- Slow physics: `z_ddt_u_tot`/`z_ddt_v_tot` are interpolated from cell centers onto edge-normal components → `pt_diag%ddt_vn_phy` (mo_nh_interface_nwp.f90:2844-2853).
- Fast physics (turbulence): applied *immediately* to the state, both to diagnostic `u` (`p_diag%u += tcall_turb_jg*ddt_u_turb`, mo_nwp_turbdiff_interface.f90:916) and to the prognostic `pt_prog%vn += dt_loc*(c_lin_e·turb)` (mo_nh_interface_nwp.f90:2855-2865). This means turbulence *already* produced the `u(t+dt)` increment inside physics, unlike slow physics.

### Step 4 — Time integration consumes `ddt_vn_phy`

Driver loop: `integrate_nh` (mo_nh_stepping.f90:1922) → `perform_dyn_substepping` (:2991, called at :2182) → `solve_nh` (:3094, dt_dyn = dt_phy/ndyn_substeps).

The momentum update in `solve_nh` (mo_solve_nonhydro.f90:1363-1367):

```
z_ddt_vn_dyn = advection+Coriolis (time-averaged)
             + pressure-gradient (semi-implicit)
             + p_nh%diag%ddt_vn_phy          ← physics in dynamics
vn(nnew) = vn(nnow) + dtime * z_ddt_vn_dyn
```

Note: physics is called *after* dynamics within each `jstep` (nwp_nh_interface at mo_nh_stepping.f90:2415; dynamics at :2182), so the `ddt_vn_phy` consumed by dynamics is the one computed in the *previous* step — slow-physics tendencies are time-lagged by one physics interval.

### Step 5 — `u(t+dt)` is re-diagnosed

After integration, `u`,`v` at `nnew` are reconstructed from the updated `vn` (`rbf_vec_interpol_cell`, mo_nh_stepping.f90:2384 / :3301).

## Full call chain for `gfluxlaun → u(t+dt)`

```
data_gwd%gfluxlaun  (tunable launch momentum-flux amplitude)
  └─ gwdrag_wms (mo_gwd_wms.f90:57)              → ptenu [Pa/s, cell centers]
      └─ nwp_gwdrag (mo_nwp_gw_interface.f90:59)  → prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_gwd  [in-place scaling/limit]
          └─ nwp_nh_interface (mo_nh_interface_nwp.f90:199)
              ├─ z_ddt_u_tot = ddt_u_gwd + raylfric + ddt_u_sso + ddt_u_pconv   (:2454)
              └─ ddt_vn_phy = c_lin_e · (z_ddt_u_tot, z_ddt_v_tot)              (:2844)
                  └─ solve_nh (mo_solve_nonhydro.f90:102)
                      └─ vn(nnew) = vn(nnow) + dtime·(∇·[apc] + pgr + ddt_vn_phy) (:1367)
                          └─ rbf_vec_interpol_cell → diag%u, diag%v (mo_nh_stepping.f90:3301)
                              → u(t+dt)
```

## `.dot` data-flow graph

```dot
digraph phy2wind {
  rankdir=LR;
  node [shape=box, fontname="monospace", fontsize=9];

  gfluxlaun [label="gfluxlaun\ndata_gwd (tunable)"];
  gwdram ["gwdrag_wms\nmo_gwd_wms.f90:57\n=> ptenu (cell, Pa/s)"];
  gwdrag ["nwp_gwdrag\nmo_nwp_gw_iface.f90:59\n=> prm_nwp_tend%ddt_u_gwd\n(in-place: scale, limit, SPPT)"];
  turb   ["nwp_turbdiff\n=> ddt_u_turb | ddt_v_turb"];
  sso    ["nwp_gwdrag\n=> ddt_u_sso (+ ddt_v_sso)"];
  conv   ["nwp_convection\n=> ddt_u_pconv (+ ddt_v_pconv)"];
  rayl   ["Rayleigh friction\nnwp_nh_iface.f90:2431\n=> zddt_u_raylfric"];
  acc    ["z_ddt_u_tot = gwd+rayl+sso+pconv\nmo_nh_iface.f90:2454\n(local scratch)"];
  iproj  ["ddt_vn_phy = c_lin_e·(u_tot,v_tot)\nmo_nh_iface.f90:2844\n(u,v,cell) -> (vn,edge)"];
  solve  ["solve_nh\nmo_solve_nonhydro.f90:102\nvn(nnew)=vn(nnow)+dt·(apc+pgr+ddt_vn_phy)\n:1367"];
  uout   ["rbf_vec_interpol_cell\n(vn,edge) -> (u,v,cell)\nmo_nh_stepping.f90:3301"];

  gfluxlaun -> gwdram;
  gwdram -> gwdrag;
  gwdrag -> acc;  turb -> turb; turb -> iproj [style=dashed,label="direct vn update :2855"];
  sso -> acc; conv -> acc; rayl -> acc;
  acc -> iproj;
  iproj -> solve;
  solve -> uout;
  uout [label="diag%u, diag%v\n= u(t+dt)"];

  turbf [label="nucl. turb. direct\np_diag%u += dt·ddt_u_turb\nmo_nwp_turbdiff:916"];
  turb -> turbf [style=dashed];
  turbf -> uout [style=dashed, label="u updated in-place\ninside physics"];
}
```

## Complications for autodifferentiation (gfluxlaun → u(t+dt))

1. **State-space round trip mangles the chain**: `ddt_u_gwd` lives on cell centers in `(u,v)`; the dynamics only integrates `vn` (edges); output requires going back to `(u,v)`. Differentiating requires threading through both the projection `c_lin_e` *and* the RBF reconstruction — two nonlinear geometric maps, each row-locally dependent on grid geometry.
2. **Time-lagged coupling**: slow-physics tendencies set at step *j* are consumed by dynamics at step *j+1*, while turbulence (fast physics) updates state immediately. The AD tape must capture this ordering or the Jacobian is wrong even though component-wise each map is smooth.
3. **In-place accumulation / module-global state**: `prm_nwp_tend` is a persistent `TARGET` container; schemes scale/limit/perturb tendencies in place (mo_nwp_gw_interface.f90:268-281). For reverse-mode AD you need a custom pullback, not just tape on `ddt_u_gwd`.
4. **Substepping + predictor–corrector**: dynamics runs `ndyn_substeps` times per physics step, each with 2 stages (Wicker–Skamarock-style); physics enters BOTH predictor (:1410) and corrector (:1367) — the correction multiplies the physics contribution's derivative.
5. **Half-level/block architecture**: physics (block-cyclic over `jc`), dynamics (edge-centric), plus `nproma` blocking and MPI/OpenACC offload — tools must handle `block-index` iteration and halo regions (`rl_start/rl_end`).
6. **Diagnosed vs prognostic path**: `diag%u` is *also* updated directly by turbulence inside physics, so there are physically two different `u(t+dt)` representations; the "final" model `u` comes from the `vn` reconstruction.

If you want, next I can identify the *smallest* differentiable envelope (e.g. SCM / single-column mode where the horizontal-geometry maps collapse, or the `add_slowphys_scm` path at mo_nh_stepping.f90:2202) that still spans `gfluxlaun → u(t+dt)`, which is the natural AD boundary for your first (physics-tendency) gradient.
